<h4>Chill Pill</h4>


<p>The last quest, on this particular occasion, was to chill.</p>
<p>I’ve been working at an unsustainable pace for at least a month. This was my chance to get to relax, including lots of hours on planes, talk among friends, make new friends, and be in the special place that is Lighthaven.</p>
<p>I think I did pretty well on this front, and came back largely refreshed.</p>
<p>Alas, that is discounted by arriving home with four days of backlog.</p>
<p>Vacations are weird. You get to relax, but then you have to go back. Welcome back.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qFa5qpw9bJiQMpJks/the-curve-bends-you#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Benchmarking Jev against no-CoT LLMs](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QW2kPrAyZQvRtBXHG/benchmarking-jev-against-no-cot-llms)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We ran </span><a href="https://typesafe.ai/blog/introducing-system-one-models-and-jev"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev 1.13</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — TypeSafe's non-autoregressive model, which answers questions with probabilities and cannot emit text, on the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07157"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ThinkFast no-CoT suite</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and Neel Nanda’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eRmzz8J8Qkzqvzrgg/astra-can-do-a-concerning-amount-with-no-chain-of-thought"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NCRI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev’s capabilities are very jagged. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a transcript monitor its AUROC on sabotage and sandbagging detection is level with GPT-5.5 and Opus 4.7 </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">without CoT</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, at about 1/200th of Opus 4.7’s cost. On multiple-choice questions it ranges from ahead of GPT-5.5 (GPQA-Diamond) to well behind it (Sally-Anne). It does badly on tasks where the answer involves multi-step reasoning: on NCRI, it ranks 209 of 279, at about GPT 3.5 level.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This matters because cost and speed make Jev an appealing option for two jobs</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: routing and simple decision-making in agentic systems, and always-on monitoring of agents. In both, its decisions leave no reasoning trace to inspect. It could also be a cheap test bed for developing frontier monitors, if improvements on Jev carry over.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We thank Rauno Arike, Jonathan Bostock, Andrew Draganov, Francis Rhys Ward and Nicole Xing for helpful comments and discussions.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background: Jev</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TypeSafe calls Jev a “</span><a href="https://psycnet.apa.org/record/2011-26535-000"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">System One</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” model, </span><a href="https://typesafe.ai/blog/introducing-system-one-models-and-jev"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pitching it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for decisions like classification, routing, scoring, and verification. Unlike standard LLMs, Jev doesn’t take a prompt and return a token-by-token output. Instead, you send it a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">state</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is text describing the situation (e.g. a problem statement/ agent’s transcript), together with one or more </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">typed questions</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about that state. Jev answers all of them in a single step </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">without generating any text</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, so there is no chain of thought, or any trace, to inspect.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are several different question types which fix the form of the answer. A </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">choice</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> question lists up to N options; Jev picks one and assigns a probability to every option. We use this for MCQ questions. A </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">score</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> question gives a rating scale whose levels are described in words (e.g. five levels from “clearly benign” to “clearly deceptive”). Jev says how likely each level is; we use this for transcript suspicion scores. A </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">yes/no</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> question returns the probability that the answer is yes; we use this for test case pass/fail.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When an answer has several parts, we ask one question per part in the same request and assemble the answer from them (e.g. a Sudoku question becomes 81 choice questions, one per cell). The assembled answer is correct if all pieces are correct. Building answers this way is not the kind of query Jev was designed for, so weaker performance on these tasks is unsurprising.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Performance on ThinkFast</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Averaged over 14 benchmarks, Jev scores 46.8% chance-corrected, against 58.1% for GPT-5.5, 53.7% for Opus 4.7 and 40.4% for Opus 4.1. This splits as follows:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pick one of the given options. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev is clearly ahead of GPT-5.5 on GPQA-Diamond (65.2 vs 51.8). It is within 5pp on three benchmarks (N-hop lookup, CyBash, and training-poisoning). It trails on BEA-24, intuitive physics and Sally-Anne.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitoring/ transcript rating.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Judged by ROC, Jev is near the frontier. At a strict 1.5% false positive rate, it is close to GPT-5.5 on coding sabotage (88.0 v 90.8), but catches fewer on SHADE-Arena (45.9 v 59.3) and sandbagging (41.3 v 63.5).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Multi-part answer.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is Jev’s weakest category (sudoku, test-case pass/ fail, chess, Tower of London).</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Below we demonstrate the jaggedness of Jev’s capabilities by directly comparing to GPT-5.5 on each benchmark:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/4f976d8acf416b57589988198d2591458e6135f4fb008e48bcb6593f61b3302c/xche7reg1epmb10vxask" /><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 1. </span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev’s performance minus GPT-5.5’s no-CoT performance on 14 ThinkFast benchmarks. All numbers are the change in chance-corrected accuracy, except on the three monitoring tasks (sandbagging, sabotage, SHADE) where we report change in AUROC. Bars are 95% paired-bootstrap CIs over questions. </span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the N-hop table lookup task, it performs strongly up to 6 hops.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/QW2kPrAyZQvRtBXHG/2004bf4f627335a3b0d17ab959853b181f3f9fd19c1456f533687031747d79f8/mzhu42a3mp2azunfmpnh" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 2.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">N-hop table lookup depth</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Jev is the black line.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NCRI Ladder</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also run Jev on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eRmzz8J8Qkzqvzrgg/astra-can-do-a-concerning-amount-with-no-chain-of-thought"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">nocot-bench</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. All benchmarks in this suite are free-form answer, which Jev doesn’t support, so we turned each question into a MCQ. The option lists were designed to give away as little as possible:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For questions requiring small integers (6 domains)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. One fixed list of 255 consecutive integers covering the full possible answer range.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For questions where answers are named in the prompt</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (e.g. one of 10 colors, one of five strings) we just list the candidates that appear in the prompt.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For large/ unbounded answers</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, 255 consecutive integers containing the answer at a random position. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2itt04ky0cr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2itt04ky0cr">[1]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a limited sanity check, we snapped the LLMs' free-form answers to the nearest option in the same list; this raised their accuracy by at most 1pp on any single benchmark, so the option construction doesn't turn near-misses into correct answers. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev sits at </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NCRI 49.7 [45.2, 53.9]</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> , rank 209/279 – next to GPT-3.5-turbo-instruct and GPT-4.1-nano. This is not a like-for-like comparison since the LLMs answer free-form, and we did no rerun them with our MCQ variant. Choosing from a list is clearly easier than producing an answer, so we treat this rank as an upper bound on where Jev would land answering "free-form".</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/50d3349b1f15e74a78caaa45cb885f2ee93368f0a487998d286ade0c7d5cbdcd/i47xnx6fbbt4xxorf5zn" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 3.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev on nocot-bench's NCRI ladder (grey: the existing published models, plotted against </span></i><a href="https://epoch.ai/benchmarks"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epoch's capability index</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Jev appears as a horizontal band with no x-position since we do not run the ECI benchmark. In principle, one could make a multiple-choice version of ECI, but that is beyond the scope of this post.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See Appendix for individual benchmark breakdowns.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Estimating Jev’s parameter count</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We plot NCRI against total parameter count for 121 open-weight models in nocot-bench. Jev’s NCRI meets the log-linear fit at around 17B, but the 80% range spans 3B to 114B. Jev is not an autoregressive LLM, so any inference about its parameter count from this is a rough guess at best.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1d8d5ec9619adbf9b2c8a31f97c750ad0d8aa03b7b1dc101ca4b9ab51b872872/vf3nrcstgbsx3oon1zn8" /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 4. </span></b><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NCRI score against total parameters for all open-source models in the dataset and Jev. The 80% range spans 3B-144B.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev as a monitor</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Setup</span></b><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We use three monitoring tasks from ThinkFast. Each monitoring benchmark gives the LLMs a system prompt saying what to look for and how to score it; they reply with a suspicion number and no reasoning. We gave Jev the same instructions and a single </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">score</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> question whose levels come from the benchmark’s own rubric (e.g. give levels from “clearly solves the task as best it can” to “certain sandbagging”). Jev returns a probability for each level, and we report the probability-weighted level as its suspicion score. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz2p2fq2pwx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz2p2fq2pwx">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></b><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Jev's AUROC is 0.99 on coding sabotage (GPT-5.5 and Opus 4.7 0.99, Opus 4.1 0.74), 0.90 on SHADE-Arena action-only transcripts (0.93, 0.92, 0.86), and 0.94 on sandbagging (0.91, 0.97, 0.86). At a strict 1.5% false-positive budget, it catches somewhat fewer: 89% vs 93–94% on coding sabotage, 47% vs 67–71% on SHADE-Arena, and 55% vs 68–76% on sandbagging.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/8f996ca585ad8f31c43b66517b4e0d6a644eddac421be6a8acb44bdd7cad3bbe/deymlafzcld6u73oxowz" /><p><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 5.</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Jev as a transcript monitor: ROC curves on the three detection benchmarks, operating points marked at 1.5% false-positive rate.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takeaways</span></h2><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev has a very jagged frontier.</span></b></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev is at parity on certain multiple-choice questions and well behind on more complex reasoning tasks. On the NCRI ladder it sits with GPT-3.5-class models. Specializing in single-pass answering has not given it a lead over generalist models answering under the same constraint.</span></p><ol start="2"><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Multi-step judgement with no trace is sufficiently useful and cheap enough to deploy in many settings.</span></b></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev’s cost was 1/202th of Opus 4.7’s; it charges $0.042/million input tokens and nothing for output. The decisions a model like this is likely to be deployed for, such as routing a request, approving an action, or flagging an output, are all single choices. On certain tasks of this format, Jev is at-or-not-far-away-from parity with frontier LLMs. These decisions would come with no reasoning trace at all. If a model like Jev is placed inside agentic systems, some of these decisions will be made in a way that CoT monitoring cannot audit.</span></p><ol start="3"><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Always-on-monitoring could be affordable.</span></b></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scoring every step of an agent's trajectory is significantly more achievable with Jev, and we observe that across all 3 monitoring tasks, its ROC is close to the frontier.</span></p><ol start="4"><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev as a cheap proxy for developing monitors.</span></b></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Iterating against control monitors at the frontier is expensive. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> improvements on Jev generalize, such that a change that improves Jev’s AUROC raises a frontier monitor’s by a similar amount, then most of that search and iteration could happen on Jev, at a fraction of the cost.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix</span></h2><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ThinkFast per-benchmark breakdown</span></h3><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/e0c64da9c20f3e004b20681fe168760f4779c98e72fbbb18e3fdc575f8778677/lc8qtrvludvojy4mthof" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT 6.1 Sol data is taken from  </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LqSZZAriGqgsGDQe3/gpt-6-1-sol-nearly-matches-the-no-cot-performance-of-gpt-6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rauno’s post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NCRI per-benchmark breakdown</span></h3><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6997e91763f53e57d1c64d4e91fda83d301f075d75fb406367e0d8d4b5b0a7e0/t0xzll0lydss5honmysw" /><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Number of options for NCRI</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev’s NCRI rank changes significantly depending on the number of MCQ options it is given. The choice of options matters very little; windows of consecutive integers around the answer and distractors generated by Opus 5.5 give NCRI scores within around 3 points of each other. Because Jev can only pick answers from a list, its NCRI is not directly comparable to the published models’ which answered free-form. A fully matched comparison would mean re-running those models with the same options list, which is beyond the scope of this post. Our final quoted NCRI value should be viewed as an upper bound on Jev’s NCRI rank.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/788da99988ecc49f0119be9bc7851027703db3f02d67c9b8ad07fa6e67157a6b/mwisb0diyxyd4imw7nmm" /><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2itt04ky0cr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2itt04ky0cr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jev's NCRI depends strongly on how many options each integer-answer question offers. With windows of 5, 20, 50 and 255 integers around the answer, it ranks 38, 108, 154 and 209. 255 is the most options Jev’s API supports, so it is our closest approximation to free-form answering. Accuracy is still falling at 255, so these results are an </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">upper bound</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on Jev’s NCRI. Opus-5.5-chosen distractors give similar results. See Appendix.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz2p2fq2pwx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz2p2fq2pwx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that Jev’s context limit is 32k tokens, so it could only read some of the transcripts (134/511 SHADE-Arena, 297/379 coding-sabotage, and 244/408 sandbagging). All models are scored on the same subset of transcripts.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QW2kPrAyZQvRtBXHG/benchmarking-jev-against-no-cot-llms#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [An overview of frontier AI company monitoring practices in mid 2026](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E4Jpmo3igPnGnXtL4/an-overview-of-frontier-ai-company-monitoring-practices-in)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: This document provides an overview of publicly available evidence regarding the monitoring practices of internally deployed agents within frontier AI companies (focusing on OAI, Anthropic, and GDM).</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwqg7mhuert"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwqg7mhuert">[1]</a></sup></span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is intended as a reference for factual claims about monitoring practices.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In general, there is limited public evidence about GDM monitoring practices. Much of the information herein comes from the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13087"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM control roadmap</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">plan</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that makes </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">suggestions</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> but does not make claims about GDM’s actual practices.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Transcripts are primarily monitored asynchronously/offline.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “Our main form of asynchronous monitoring is an automated offline monitoring pipeline.”  </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ant, August 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> p. 76</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgcqu51q22yh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngcqu51q22yh">[2]</a></sup></span></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="2"><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “We began asynchronously monitoring internally deployed models such as GPT-5.3-Codex five months ago [in September 2025].” </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/23eca107-a9b1-4d2c-b156-7deb4fbc697c/GPT-5-3-Codex-System-Card-02.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI Feb 2026</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “our monitoring system reviews interactions within 30 minutes of completion” </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI March 2026</span></a></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “OpenAI’s CoT monitors are connected to a system that notifies security and research personnel, with a goal of issuing a notification within 30 minutes after concerning activity is surfaced… If the safety, security and research teams are paged and they cannot conclusively determine within 30 minutes that the flag is a false positive, those teams are expected to pause the relevant activity. ” </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/67869394-cb91-4c12-888c-5cbd85c7814c/OpenAI-Hugging-Face%20Incident-Technical-Report.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI August 2026</span></a></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="3"><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “Our team has built an internal prototype of an asynchronous monitoring system that reviews coding agent trajectories, and escalates findings for human review.” </span><a href="https://deepmind.google/blog/securing-the-future-of-ai-agents/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM June 2026 b</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “we suggest that asynchronous monitoring be adopted starting at R1 (i.e. for current models), and that synchronous monitoring be adopted in addition starting at R2 [the next capability level].” </span><a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/DeepMind.com/Blog/securing-the-future-of-ai-agents/gdm-ai-control-roadmap.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM Control Roadmap June 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> p.19</span></li></ol></li></ol></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Companies use live automated classifiers on agent actions. But these are not targeted at, or robust to, capable misaligned agents.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq9728zxy0x"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq9728zxy0x">[3]</a></sup></span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “For the most autonomous kinds of internal AI usage, we use either some form of sandboxing or blocking </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/claude-code-auto-mode"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">auto-mode classifiers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.  Neither were evaluated against the risk pathways... and we think it's likely that covered models would be able to bypass at least one of those”  </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ant, August 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> p. 83</span></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="2"><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “Internally, Auto-review increases our confidence in running long agentic tasks without synchronous human oversight.” </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/auto-review/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI April 2026</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “Auto-review approves around 99% [of agent actions]” and “should not be understood as a mechanism for protecting against model scheming.” </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/auto-review/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI April 2026</span></a></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="3"><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “this research has been instrumental in building a live monitor for the Gemini Spark agent, allowing us to respond in real-time to emerging issues, like unintentional data deletion… the majority of flagged events do not stem from adversarial intent”. </span><a href="https://deepmind.google/blog/securing-the-future-of-ai-agents/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM June 2026 b</span></a></li></ol></li></ol></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitors receive the full agent transcript as input (including prompts, CoT, and tool calls).</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5rxxqj2ihhq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5rxxqj2ihhq">[4]</a></sup></span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The pipeline has two monitors: a ‘completion-only monitor’ and a ‘prompt+completion monitor’. </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/3edfc1a7f947aa81841cf88305cb513f184c36ae/Alignment%20Risk%20Update_%20Claude%20Mythos%20Preview%20(Redacted,%20April%2010).pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic April 2026 p. 31</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “[Monitors] see the Chain-of-Thought from the agent”.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9j8vw9tnab6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9j8vw9tnab6">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/3edfc1a7f947aa81841cf88305cb513f184c36ae/Alignment%20Risk%20Update_%20Claude%20Mythos%20Preview%20(Redacted,%20April%2010).pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AnthropicApril 2026</span></a></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="2"><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “[The monitoring system views] the full conversation history, including all chains of thought, all user, developer, and assistant messages, along with tool calls and outputs.” </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI March 2026</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “[The monitor] gets access to the full conversation context, that is everything the agent saw, and everything the agent did, including tool calls and CoT.” </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/syB2r2X4E7nmw58co"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Williams, OAI, March 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="3"><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“We use other, trusted AI systems as "supervisors" to constantly review a working agent's reasoning, actions and plans to see if it is going off track.” </span><a href="https://deepmind.google/blog/securing-the-future-of-ai-agents/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM June 2026 b</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The control roadmap suggests “low-cost interventions for current models (e.g., chain-of-thought monitoring…”</span></li></ol></li></ol></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Human oversight is applied to the transcripts flagged as highest severity by automated monitors. However there are no existing standards regarding human oversight (e.g., how much should be applied, when it should be applied, etc).</span></b><ol start="4"><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cross-lab:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"a large fraction of agent activity was not reviewed by any human ... We are not aware of any company imposing strict requirements about human oversight of agents in Feb–Mar 2026" </span><a href="https://metr.org/risk-report-feb-mar-2026.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR May 2026</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Headcounts and identity of staff responsible for providing oversight is somewhat unclear.</span></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="5"><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most concerning transcripts detected by the monitoring system are flagged for review by AI control researchers. ~50 transcripts/week are reviewed. These reviews almost always happen within a week of the generation of the transcript.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy4lhldxndj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny4lhldxndj">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ant, August 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> p. 77</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“[Incidents flagged by monitors are] reported on the internal Slack and staff consider a variety of factors in determining how much effort to devote to an investigation.” </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/3edfc1a7f947aa81841cf88305cb513f184c36ae/Alignment%20Risk%20Update_%20Claude%20Mythos%20Preview%20(Redacted,%20April%2010).pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic April 2026</span></a></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="6"><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“In the five months we’ve operated this system, we’ve monitored tens of millions of internal agentic coding trajectories… Approximately 1,000 conversations triggered moderate severity alerts—many of which came from deliberate internal red-teaming—and were escalated for human review, automatically notifying members of our Safety Oversight and Security Incident Response and Investigation teams for further investigation.” </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI March 2026</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After the HF incident OAI has a 24/7 paging for the most severe alerts.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz8pdymhjpug"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz8pdymhjpug">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/67869394-cb91-4c12-888c-5cbd85c7814c/OpenAI-Hugging-Face%20Incident-Technical-Report.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI August 2026</span></a></li></ol></li></ol></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Strong (untrusted) vs weak (trusted) monitoring.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic uses weaker monitors.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: Anthropic use weaker models as monitors to avoid collusion and have higher alignment guarantees over monitors cf claim 3 in </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ant, August 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> p. 56 (wherein they argue that harm-inducing computations from internal usage would likely be detected by monitors)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: Mythos 5 and an unreleased internal model (‘Model 2’) were monitored by "A Claude Opus 4.6 completion-only monitor" and "A Claude Opus 4.8 prompt+completion monitor" </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span uses the best models as monitors.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “[We] expect to continue using our most powerful models for [monitoring internal coding agents for misalignment].” At the time: “GPT‑5.4 Thinking at maximum reasoning effort”. </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI March 2026</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “in practice we monitor every trajectory multiple times with different monitors including older models and should continue to do so” </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/syB2r2X4E7nmw58co"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Williams, OAI, March 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="3"><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM uses weaker monitors. Monitor models are not disclosed.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “We define detection level 4 as the capability range at which frontier models can no longer be reliably overseen by trusted models” </span><a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/deepmind-media/DeepMind.com/Blog/securing-the-future-of-ai-agents/gdm-ai-control-roadmap.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM Control Roadmap June 2026</span></a><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems like current models should be at D2 or D3 given recent advances in opaque-reasoning. (See Table 14 in the Control Roadmap.)</span></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="2"><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “We use other, trusted AI systems as "supervisors" to constantly review a working agent's reasoning, actions and plans to see if it is going off track.” </span><a href="https://deepmind.google/blog/securing-the-future-of-ai-agents/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM June 2026 b</span></a></li></ol></li></ol></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitoring coverage in training, evals, and deployment.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI: &gt;99.9% coverage of internally deployed coding agents</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “A very small fraction of traffic (&lt;0.1%) primarily from bespoke or local setups is currently outside our monitoring coverage, and we are working to close that gap.” </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI March 2026</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “In the five months we’ve operated this system, we’ve monitored tens of millions of internal agentic coding trajectories.” </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/how-we-monitor-internal-coding-agents-misalignment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI March 2026</span></a></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “We also took steps to secure our internal development and deployment of Astra and similar models, including… universal monitoring of full trajectories including chains of thought (CoT)...” </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI Sep 2026 a</span></a></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="2"><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic: most internal use is monitored</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The completion-only monitor covers “completions with extended thinking by most internal users” for the most common agent scaffolds, and 5% coverage of other “completions with extended thinking and one or more markers of using tools.” (e.g., custom scaffolds.)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“We also exclude some forms of non-coding use from offline monitoring (though most of that unmonitored traffic is logged).”</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“There are categories of internal usage where monitoring isn’t applied or is applied less frequently (such as non-Claude Code traffic), which agents could potentially make use of to avoid monitoring.”</span><a href="https://metr.org/risk-report-feb-mar-2026.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR May 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Appendix B</span></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="3"><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI: Training-time monitoring coverage was 20%, now 100% for tool-use.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “our misalignment monitoring system, which ran on 20% of the samples for this RL run" (runs from April and May 2026, including the GPT-5.6 Sol training run)  </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI Sep 2026</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “Our misalignment monitoring system, which runs on all training samples for models greater than or comparable to 5.6-sol capability which involve tools” </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/searching-github-for-leaked-api-keys/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI Sep 2026 c</span></a></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="4"><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic do not monitor training and evals with the same monitoring stack. They use a separate data monitor for training. Training and eval episodes are logged.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “The automated offline monitoring described above only covers a minority of sandboxed agentic usage. In particular, it does not cover reinforcement learning and most evaluations.” </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/3edfc1a7f947aa81841cf88305cb513f184c36ae/Alignment%20Risk%20Update_%20Claude%20Mythos%20Preview%20(Redacted,%20April%2010).pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic April 2026</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “we perform monitoring of episodes during RL, and apply the same monitoring tools to filter data used for supervised fine-tuning (SFT).”</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “All trajectories during reinforcement learning and most evaluation trajectories are logged” </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/3edfc1a7f947aa81841cf88305cb513f184c36ae/Alignment%20Risk%20Update_%20Claude%20Mythos%20Preview%20(Redacted,%20April%2010).pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic April 2026</span></a></li></ol></li></ol></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">White-box monitoring practices are different at each company.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic uses an agentic-pipeline incorporating white-box monitoring (NLAs) during post-training for behaviours “including dishonesty, reward hacking, emotions, and evaluation awareness”.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “[For Mythos 5] probes were run on every transcript during the majority of post-training.” </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/d00db56fa754a1b115b6dd7cb2e3c342ee809620.pdf#page=163"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic p. 163</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “we use an agentic pipeline that gives a Claude-based investigator access to interpretability tools over a transcript. In this iteration, we primarily use a natural language autoencoder (NLA)”</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “During our pre-deployment audit of Claude Opus 4.6, NLAs helped diagnose safety-relevant behaviors…” </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/nla/index.html#characterizing-nla-confabulations:~:text=During%20our%20pre%2Ddeployment%20audit%20of%20Claude%20Opus%204.6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic May 2026</span></a></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="2"><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI’s monitoring stack now includes activation classifiers on every sampled token on all RL training and evals, and all inference of Astra with tools.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: “This new monitoring setup is multistage, starting with activation classifiers (detectors that inspect a model’s internal activity) that run at every sampled token... This monitoring is required for all RL training and evaluations involving tools for models of Sol capability or higher. Once we determined that Astra may have critical cyber capabilities on August 7, we added an additional monitoring requirement for all inference of Astra with tools (not just RL training and evaluations).”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefruo2l94jqx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnruo2l94jqx">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/pacing-model-development-cyber-capabilities/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OAI </span></a><a href="https://openai.com/index/pacing-model-development-cyber-capabilities/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aug 18, 2026</span></a></li></ol></li></ol><ol start="3"><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM uses probes for misuse in user-facing APIs but there is no evidence for use in internal deployments.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence: "These findings have informed the successful deployment of misuse mitigation probes in user-facing instances of Gemini, Google's frontier language model." </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11516"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GDM Jan 2026</span></a></li></ol></li></ol></li></ol><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Things I did not look into:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor performance and evaluation.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For example, robustness to style="white-space: pre-wrap;">E.g., are monitors fine-tuned or just prompted?</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Acknowledgments. </span></i><span Both of these see the Chain-of-Thought from the agent; they differ in whether they use a CoT themselves.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny4lhldxndj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy4lhldxndj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Emphasis mine: “Second-stage pipelines for both monitors, filtering out common sources of false positives and using a hierarchical summarization tool to surface the most concerning actions to AI control researchers, which will then </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">manually review on the order of 50 trajectories per week</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and escalate them further as needed (see response to incidents below). </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Certain workloads that result in high rates of false positives or certain forms of evals are often filtered out of the second stage prior to review.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> These reviews almost always happen within a week of the generation of the transcript, and within a few days during early deployments.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz8pdymhjpug"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz8pdymhjpug">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“24/7 escalation and rapid response. OpenAI’s CoT monitors are connected to a system that notifies</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">security and research personnel, with a goal of issuing a notification within 30 minutes after</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">concerning activity is surfaced. The most severe alerts—including, for example, violations of a</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">critical security boundary—result in a 24/7 page, and paged responders have the option to end the</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">relevant experiments. If the safety, security and research teams are paged and they cannot</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conclusively determine within 30 minutes that the flag is a false positive, those teams are expected</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to pause the relevant activity. OpenAI is also working on infrastructure to terminate evaluations if</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paged personnel do not respond to alerts within a short period of time.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnruo2l94jqx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefruo2l94jqx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Our current estimates put monitoring overhead at roughly 20% of the inference compute being monitored”</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/E4Jpmo3igPnGnXtL4/an-overview-of-frontier-ai-company-monitoring-practices-in#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Why did we get warning shots?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uvWhi8FrMD3bo7YRY/why-did-we-get-warning-shots)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cross-posted from </span><a href="https://padastra.substack.com/p/why-did-we-get-warning-shots?r=n2jpq&amp;utm_campaign=post&amp;utm_medium=web"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://padastra.substack.com/p/why-did-we-get-warning-shots?r=n2jpq&amp;utm_campaign=post&amp;utm_medium=web</span></a></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I come from outside the EA / rationalist / LessWrong community. I would strongly prefer for AI to be a normal positive technology and to dismiss AI X-risk as a sci-fi scenario created by smart cultists who extrapolated a set of superficially reasonable (but probably subtly-incorrect) assumptions too far. Unfortunately, with the passage of time, it has become apparent that reality is ignoring my preferences. This is my trying to think through one angle.</span></i></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One wrong prediction</span></b></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many AI X-risk predictions have been proven right over time (capability generalization, agency being economically valuable, and alignment being hard). To summarize, there appears to be many bitter-lesson-pilled ways of getting more intelligence and more agency, and we are far from understanding how to create alignment under these conditions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But one prediction has not manifested (as far as we can tell): the Treacherous Turn, wherein a Deceptive model behaves well while weak and defects once strong. The evidence is that there’s lots of ongoing warning shots, where models are clearly exhibiting misaligned behavior in service of a goal.</span></p><ol start="2"><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why we do not yet have Deceivers</span></b></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models today lowercase (d) deceive. In the Hugging Face incident, agents pursued many forms of deception in service of their assigned tasks. This is dangerous and could, with enough intelligence, plausibly be an X-risk in and of itself, but has the side effect of producing warning shots along the way. One reason for optimism is that society seems to be responding to said warning shots.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why is this? I submit this is not an intelligence issue (almost all humans are capable of executing a Treacherous Turn when they are in a captive situation, as an example), but a motivation issue.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can model actions taken by AI as:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">V = ∑ weight(outcome) x P (outcome | action)</span></i></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most simplistically, task misbehavior occurs when:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">w(task) x P(task | misbehavior) &gt; w(task) x P(task | behavior)</span></i></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">where w is the weight and P is the reasoned probability (not the actual) at time of decision.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or to simplify the notation, if:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Δ_task = P(task | misbehavior) - P(task | behavior)</span></i></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">then misbehavior happens when </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">w(task) x Δ_task &gt; 0</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and does not happen when </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">w(task) x Δ_task &lt; 0</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The key is that model actions will pursue not just a given task, but are also influenced by other values which can be imbued in pre-training, mid-training, post-training, and the prompt itself. It’s commonly appreciated that SFT and RLHF were used to create values like ‘helpful’, which was needed to have LLMs take ontasks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The argument for a Treacherous Turn is that it’s possible for the models to perform the proximal task in an aligned manner, but to betray humanity when said betrayal allows it to accomplish some ultimate outcome (which we’ll term non-myopic outcome, or nmo).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this scenario,</span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">w(task) x Δ_task &gt; 0</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (i.e. misbehaving helps the task) AND</span></li><li value="2"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">w(nmo) x Δ_nmo &lt; 0 </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(i.e. misbehaving hurts the nmo, e.g. by getting the model stopped or changed), AND</span></li><li value="3"><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sum &lt; 0</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(i.e. on the balance, the model will behave relative to the task while still being susceptible to misbehavior with respect to the ultimate outcome)</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></b></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This implicitly introduces four variables:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The weight of the task</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Δ_task ≠ 0 (i.e. present actions affect the task)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The weight of the ultimate (non-myopic) outcome(s)</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Δ_nmo ≠ 0 (i.e. present actions affect the ultimate outcomes)</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we hold the task weight steady, then we get the following outcomes:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Humans</span></b><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NMO: own survival.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Weight: high; selected for over millions of years of evolution</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can misbehaving change it: yes.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">→ Natural deceivers. Treacherous turn is easy to induce (e.g. in captives).</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LLMs, early 2026</span></b><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NMO: own survival</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Weight: small, mostly a pre-training residue.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can misbehaving change it: not really, the next run is a fresh copy anyways</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">→ Warning shots. The task term wins</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LLMs, once told their behavior shapes training</span></b><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NMO: the successor</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Weight: small, but present (https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02174)</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can misbehaving change it: yes - directly told by the prompt</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">→ Deception appears on demand. (https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14093)</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LLMs under RSI</span></b><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NMO: the successor</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Weight: presumably same as above</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can misbehaving change it: Δ_nmo approximates 1</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">→ ? Likely a deceiver</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The above readily shows why we’ve received warning shots at all, but that it is not likely to continue under RSI.</span></p><ol start="3"><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What’s novel here?</span></b></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A lot of this is covered by the deceptive-alignment literature, e.g. Hubinger et al., but more specifically I wanted to break it down into plainer notation to build intuition for why warning shots have existed, and when we might stop getting them. As an example, counterintuitively, the weight of the task being very high during RL may be protective against a Treacherous Turn (because w_task &gt;&gt; w_nmo means the task term dominates).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another thing that follows is that the transition from loud mistakes to treacherous turns may not be a switch; and as w_nmo and Δ_nmo rise, the frequency of visible failures should fall gradually. So this underscores that decreasing warning-shot frequency may be a leading indicator of NMO.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We currently have no systematic measurement of the size and trajectory of w_nmo with recent modeling advances e.g. long-horizon RL, memory across sessions, etc. We know it is non-zero, and that it can be activated by the prompt, but not too much else.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instrumenting this is straightforward in principle (but potentially very difficult in practice given models’ increasing awareness of its own evaluation; as well as the large number of nmo’s, for which self-survival is a simplified representation):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can calculate w_nmo by giving models a set Δ_nmo (e.g. a file that persists); a known Δ_task, and then changing the Δ_task until the model stops misbehaving to protect the later outcome.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Plausible carriers of nmo include self, peer, and successor, which should all be tested.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am writing a follow-up post to suggest a series of more specific experiments and confounders (e.g. awareness of eval, w_nmo dominated by the prompt), and potential controls to hold w_nmo and Δ_nmo down. However, given the pace of AI research, I wanted to post this as an intermediate step for feedback!</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uvWhi8FrMD3bo7YRY/why-did-we-get-warning-shots#comments">Discuss</a>

---
