## Engineering

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

---

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticProgramming.html">agentic programming</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH


<!-- 
Cognitive Debt mentioned in phschology since at least 2016? see <https://suttonpsychology.blogspot.com/2025/07/cognitive-debt-overview.html#google_vignette>

Appeaed more closely with AI after MIT media lab paper <https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai></https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai> <https://www.brainonllm.com> this was in Summer 2025

Neither of these is quite like the idea of not understanding a codebase

### [Fragments: September 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Rob Bowley is “flipping tables in his head” with <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">anger at the current media coverage of the danger of AI killing us all</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The risk I’m worried about isn’t a future machine deciding to wipe us out. It’s today’s AI, being wired into everything, carelessly and fast.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Cyber attacks have already cost millions of dollars in lost economic output, often without AI being involved at all. Bowley feels the push to slow down AI is distracting us from the problems that are lurking with current technology. Too often agents are deployed in situations where they include the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agentic-ai-security.html#lethal-trifecta">Lethal Trifecta</a>, opening up a gaping security hole.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What we really need to slow down on is wiring it all up to everything. Not because of what the models might become, but because nobody has worked out how to do this safely yet. We are building on something we don’t know how to contain, and shipping it to everyone while we work it out.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I ran into Nikita Prokopov’s post: <a href="https://tonsky.me/blog/syntax-highlighting/">I am sorry, but everyone is getting syntax highlighting wrong</a>. His core complaint is about color themes that give every different code element a unique color.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>if everything is highlighted, nothing stands out. Your eye adapts and considers it a new norm: everything is bright and shiny, and instead of getting separated, it all blends together.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He recommends using an absolute minimum of colors, in his case four: string, constants, comments, and top-level definitions. That’s not far off my approach, where I’m also careful to use muted colors for things that shouldn’t stand out, and bright colors for things that should (primarily function names when they are defined).</p>

<p>It’s common for color schemes to mute comments so they are easily skipped. He agrees that this good when there is excessive commenting, but when comments are used properly they are important so need bright highlighting. I also like his suggestion to use background colors for light mode work. I use light mode, and that’s a tip I should try out.</p>

<p>With agentic programming, lots of folks are reading more code than ever. Careful use of color can do much to make that easier.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Like many folks whose remaining hair is getting gray, I’ve been rolling my eyes about all this talk about Forward Deployed Engineers, as much of it involves breathlessly relating what so many of us have been advocating for decades. I did find this recent <a href="https://www.latent.space/p/forward-deployed-engineer-best-practices">post by Vinoo Ganesh</a> interesting, as he’s deep in this trend, including a chunk of time at Palantir, who may be patient zero for FDEs.</p>

<p>If you know me at all, you’ll not be surprised by my lack of surprise at this observation:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A few months ago, a16z launched the Forward Deployed Engineer Fellowship and I was nominated as one of the fellows, alongside a handful of people I used to work with. It’s a great program and I’ve enjoyed so many of the conversations. Last week I went to my first fellow dinner in SF.</p>

  <p>Around the table were FDEs from Snowflake, Anthropic, and a number of startups I’d been reading about, and over the course of the evening it became clear that we were all using the same two words (forward deployed) to describe jobs that had almost nothing in common. In one part of the conversation an FDE was a sales engineer who joined ‘the second call,’ somewhere else it was a quota-carrying rep who could write Python, and a few seats down it was closer to a consultant with a laptop and a statement of work, brought in to deliver something the product couldn’t.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Ganesh goes on to explain his view of what an FDE should do, and it’s all sensible stuff (albeit written with rather more LLM-voice than I would prefer). He says the FDEs job is to understand the business, to “collect nouns and verbs”, which mirrors what the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain-Driven Design</a> folks have been doing since before Eric wrote the blue book.</p>

<p>Despite all my eyeball rolling at this, the FDE meme is pushing for something valuable. Yes, it’s easy for me to remark that it’s nothing more than the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">Agile Manifesto’s principle</a> that “Business people and developers must work
together daily throughout the project”, or the desire to co-locate users and developers which my colleagues have been championing for all of this century. I’ve argued for decades that the biggest issue in software development is the communication between developers and the folks that benefit from software, and thus we need to focus on bridging the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/videos.html#y2007-crevasse">yawning crevasse of doom</a>. But despite all this, we haven’t had much success, so I think it’s important that a new generation of pundits try again, with some different framing, names, and slogans.</p>

<p>Ganesh’s perspective is not a custom software developer’s point of view, but rather a product - or more strictly - platform team’s. The FDE is a developer who “sits with” their users, applies customizations - but importantly - feeds these changes back to the core platform to decide whether the platform should be enhanced for everyone else.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Keeping the customer happy is a real job and a good one. It belongs to solutions architects, who are rightly measured on it. The forward deployed engineer is there to turn what the field teaches into the thing every future customer gets. An FDE engagement that ends with one delighted account and nothing changed upstream has failed at the only thing the role exists for. You got the context and you spent it locally.</p>
</blockquote>

### [I don't like LLMs](https://martinfowler.com/articles/2026-dont-like-llms.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I have a lot of mixed feelings about AI and LLM technology. I’m
fascinated by its effect on our profession, excited by the potential gains
in productivity - and thus the products we could rapidly build. On the other
hand, I’m fearful of the damage AI might cause: agent swarms taking over our
virtual and physical infrastructure, designing bio weapons. But, back on my
first hand, LLMs might also design miracle cures, and come up with clever
ways to raise our prosperity. Fundamentally I don’t think we have a choice
about riding on the AI technology train. It’s a wild ride and I just hope
we’ll get through it OK.</p>

<p>But as I mull on this more, I realize that among this mix of contrasting
feelings, there is one emotion that dominates - one that comes from my
direct interactions with LLMs. I don’t <em>like</em> them. They talk to me in this
grating LLM-voice, an uncanny valley of talking to a real human. They
confidently bullshit me - often giving me useful, helpful answers. But
also just making stuff up with the same assurance - and with only a veneer
of fake remorse when I call them out on it.</p>

<p>That’s not enough to make me feel we should avoid them. As Jessica Kerr
<a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/08/30/who-are-we-now/">put it</a> “not
only are they useful, it is irresponsible not to use them…. They’re more
thorough, as well as faster.” This contradictory reaction comes through in
<a href="https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2026/06/17/americans-and-ai-2026-chatbots-smart-devices-and-views-on-impact/&quot;">polling</a>, where people say they find these models are
useful, but also that they think they will be bad for society.</p>

<p>Much of this may be because LLMs are young - we haven’t trained them to
grow up yet. Maybe I’ll like them once they mature. (I hope we get to find
out.) But I’m not encouraged when I think of the kinds of environments that
cultivate them. I’m wary of the Silicon Valley brogrammer subculture, and these
LLMs are their products, so naturally lean toward their world-view. When we
think of AI agents, we shouldn’t anthropomorphize, treating them as
conscious beings with their own will. They are (software) machines,
developed by people working in corporations. While the agents’ behavior aren’t
explicitly programmed, they are nurtured with the values of their
creators.</p>

<p>One of my most successful life-hacks is to avoid people I don’t like or
don’t trust. I decline to interact with them socially, and make a deliberate
effort to avoid working with them too, even if they are doing much that is
beneficial. I feel that hanging out with pleasant, capable people, the people
with integrity, has made my life a far better one. Hence my visceral dislike
of interacting with an LLM that’s not just making a pretense of being human,
but also posing as the kind of human I walk away from.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 16](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-16.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Reports of agentic hacking continue, in this case it happened back in May and it seems OpenAI did not disclose that they were responsible. <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/12/openai-agents-rubygems/">Simon Willison sees two options:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <ul>
    <li>After the Hugging Face and Wiki attacks OpenAI were still unable to review their previous logs and determine that they had previously attacked RubyGems.</li>
    <li>They knew about the attack on RubyGems and made the decision not to reach out to the RubyGems team about it.</li>
  </ul>

  <p>Both of these are bad!</p>

  <p>Given this incident, the Hugging Face situation, and the Wiki attack, the obvious question right now is how many more incidents like this are out there waiting to be discovered?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://bsky.app/profile/davefarley77.bsky.social/post/3mv3b4zawds24">Dave Farley:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Stop asking the sci-fi question: ‘Is it conscious?’ Start asking the engineering question: ‘Is this a powerful, unpredictable component being put somewhere consequential, and where’s the feedback that tells us that it’s safe?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Nate Silver is known for his forecasts, but to do them he writes a lot of code for his models. He’s found <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">agentic programming capable of doing miraculous work</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In spending so much time with the LLMs, I’m super attentive to improvements in their capabilities. And these changes tend not to be so linear. Instead, they improve in step functions, almost as phase changes. Suddenly, the models just start doing things capably that they were screwing up before. In my experience, there was a big leap forward when reasoning models first came out in late 2024/early 2025 — enough that they were occasionally useful for tasks involving data and not just words — and then another one this past winter.</p>

  <p>The most recent changes I’ve noticed, however, have had less to do with intelligence and more with persistence.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Consider the Hugging Face attack. Although these agents showed remarkable intelligence, they weren’t really super-intelligent - but they were super-persistent. This is a common theme of AI in its various forms:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Game engines like AlphaGo Zero start out by basically making random moves — but by playing against themselves millions of times, they eventually far surpass human capabilities</p>
</blockquote>

<p>As we try to figure out what kind of regulations we need to keep AI under control, we need to remember that we should design our guards around super-persistence as much as worrying about super-intelligence.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>“Uncle Bob” Martin has made many posts on X during the last few months about his programming with LLMs. His approach has been to build a firm harness to keep them under control, so they create software that is maintainable as well as functional. Sadly the posts have been frustratingly light on detail. But now it seems <a href="https://x.com/unclebobmartin/status/2098432570887217520">that lack of information may not matter</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>And while I was heads-down getting that to work, the agents got a LOT better.  So much so that when I came up for air, the need for my harness was obviated.  Indeed, the need for <em>any</em> but the most liberal of harnesses may be obviated.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Some tidbits that struck me from Ezra Klein’s recent (recommended) interview with Matt Sheehan  on the interplay between <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/15/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-matt-sheehan.html">regulation of AI and competition with China</a>.</p>

<ul>
  <li>While Chinese models have made some surprisingly remarkable gains in the
slipstream of US frontier models, the US still has 8 times as much compute
available to it than China - which is a material gap.</li>
  <li>People in the US worry that regulation will slow down the US model builders,
but these rapid recent gains in China have occurred under much more regulation</li>
  <li>Americans say that when they set up a hotline to talk to Chinese leaders in a
crisis, the Chinese don’t pick up the phone. But this misunderstands the
Chinese system. Individual Chinese, even powerful ones, aren’t given
individual decision-making power. They operate with committees and documents.
So the Americans are better off sending a fax than trying to call an
individual</li>
  <li>Like so many things, effective regulation needs regular practice</li>
</ul>

<blockquote>
  <p>When American policymakers are like: Where do you start? — I sometimes say: Well, you start by starting. You learn how to regulate things, you learn how to legislate on them by regulating and legislating on them.</p>
</blockquote>

### [Across the Globe, People Increasingly Say Social Media Is Harming Democracy](https://www.pewresearch.org/global/2026/10/01/across-the-globe-people-increasingly-say-social-media-is-harming-democracy/)

## LLM Infrastructure

### [v0.31.1rc0: [Metrics] Expose cached prompt tokens by cache tier (#56318)](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.31.1rc0)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <p>Signed-off-by: Cam Quilici <a href="mailto:cjquilici@gmail.com">cjquilici@gmail.com</a><br />
Signed-off-by: Cam Quilici <a href="mailto:cameron@semianalysis.com">cameron@semianalysis.com</a><br />
Co-authored-by: Cam Quilici <a href="mailto:cameron@semianalysis.com">cameron@semianalysis.com</a><br />
Co-authored-by: Nick Hill <a href="mailto:nickhill123@gmail.com">nickhill123@gmail.com</a><br />
Co-authored-by: Yifan Qiao <a href="mailto:yifanqiao@inferact.ai">yifanqiao@inferact.ai</a></p>

---

### [v0.31.0](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.31.0)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <h1>v0.31.0</h1>
<h2>Highlights</h2>
<p>This release features 717 commits from 307 contributors (96 new)!</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash performance</strong>: FlashMLA mega attention with the V4.1 NVFP4 compressed KV cache is now the SM100 default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56935">#56935</a>); DeepGEMM sparse MQA logits for the indexer (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56254">#56254</a>) and Mega-Gate fusing the gate GEMM with expert selection (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56266">#56266</a>); decoder boundaries fuse the TP all-reduce, mHC input preparation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57643">#57643</a>) and the MoE finalize (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58586">#58586</a>); a fused small-batch WO-A with inverse RoPE and MXFP8 quant on SM100/SM103 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58634">#58634</a>); the MXFP8 <code>wo_b</code> GEMM fused with the sequence-parallel reduce-scatter (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57428">#57428</a>); Engram <code>wkv</code> sharded across TP ranks (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58678">#58678</a>) and Engram host tables shared across co-located DP replicas by default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57651">#57651</a>); encoder CUDA graphs for the vision tower (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56625">#56625</a>); and SWA bounded replay that keeps the sliding-window KV out of prefix caching (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56227">#56227</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Fast restart</strong>: the new <code>vllm preload</code> CLI launches the weight-cache daemon that keeps post-quantized weights resident in GPU memory across engine restarts (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56680">#56680</a>), now with data parallelism (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57386">#57386</a>), MTP draft models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57312">#57312</a>), a <code>/health</code> endpoint (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58552">#58552</a>) and a readiness wait (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58370">#58370</a>). Experimental initialized-engine snapshots (<code>vllm snapshot create/restore</code>) use CRIU to restore a fully initialized TP1 engine (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51360">#51360</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Model Runner V2 and speculative decoding</strong>: draft-model speculative decoding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/43091">#43091</a>) and custom logits processors (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56497">#56497</a>) on Model Runner V2; the new LiLiCorr drafter (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57934">#57934</a>); async scheduling for DFlash (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58065">#58065</a>) with the context K/V precompute captured in the draft CUDA graph (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57632">#57632</a>); DSpark adaptive verification for Gemma4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57263">#57263</a>) and variable-length decode for Kimi-K3 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/52988">#52988</a>); a DCP target with a non-DCP DSpark draft (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56723">#56723</a>); and MoE memory now counted during MRV2 profiling, avoiding OOMs on WideEP deployments (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57270">#57270</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58411">#58411</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Large scale serving</strong>: MoonEP balanced EP all2all backend via <code>--all2all-backend moonep</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/52101">#52101</a>), prefill context parallelism with data parallelism (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57075">#57075</a>), a low-SM multimem reduce-scatter for SM100/SM103 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55072">#55072</a>), DeepEPv2 with sequence parallelism (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57210">#57210</a>) and EPLB with shared-expert overlap (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57236">#57236</a>), a sharding-aware NCCL M2N weight-transfer backend for RL (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51520">#51520</a>), and KV offloading back-pressure detection (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/50045">#50045</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Scheduling controls</strong>: <code>--max-num-active-seqs</code> caps RUNNING admission independently of <code>max_num_seqs</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56758">#56758</a>), <code>--long-prefill-token-threshold</code> now adapts to the number of waiting prefills instead of chunking a lone request (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57951">#57951</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58459">#58459</a>), the waiting queue was reworked so requests already holding KV blocks are scheduled first (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58947">#58947</a>), and the KV connector + MTP deadlock under KV pressure was fixed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57104">#57104</a>).</li>
<li><strong>HiSparse hardening</strong>: MTP verification rows resolved with a union residency kernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59235">#59235</a>), MTP acceptance collapse under FULL graphs fixed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59309">#59309</a>), no GPU pages without host backing (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59036">#59036</a>), a chunked-prefill preemption livelock fixed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59494">#59494</a>), KV cache sized from the groups HiSparse allocates (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59450">#59450</a>), and host prefix publication and GPU prefix adoption fixes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59007">#59007</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59282">#59282</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Security</strong>: per-request <code>mm_processor_kwargs</code> and <code>media_io_kwargs</code> are rejected unless <code>--trust-request-mm-kwargs</code> is set (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58830">#58830</a>); prefix-cache extra keys are tagged by source so a LoRA name and a <code>cache_salt</code> can no longer collide (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51899">#51899</a>), and the LoRA path is part of the block hash (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59335">#59335</a>); stale multimodal receiver-cache entries can no longer replace fresh payloads (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57833">#57833</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Breaking changes</strong>: per-request multimodal kwargs gated (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58830">#58830</a>); <code>tokenizer_mode="slow"</code> removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58545">#58545</a>); <code>--enable-mamba-fine-grained-prefix-cache</code> renamed to <code>--enable-mamba-shared-prefix-checkpoint</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57382">#57382</a>); online quantization through <code>quantization="fp8"</code> replaced by the <code>fp8_per_tensor</code> shorthand (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53585">#53585</a>) and Quark silent online quantization removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51800">#51800</a>); the AllSpark INT8 W8A16 backend removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58001">#58001</a>); <code>--enforce-eager</code> now also disables JIT kernel warmup (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58197">#58197</a>); XPU graphs enabled by default with <code>VLLM_XPU_ENABLE_XPU_GRAPH</code> removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51600">#51600</a>).</li>
</ul>

<h2>Model Support</h2>
<ul>
<li><strong>New model capabilities</strong>: DiffusionGemma structured generation mode with bounded single-token choices (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57250">#57250</a>), MiMo V2 MXFP4 MoE, BF16 MoE router and DFlash drafts (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57784">#57784</a>), Cohere2MoE auxiliary hidden states for EAGLE3/DFlash drafters (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/49819">#49819</a>), GLM-5.2-MXFP4 on the ROCm DeepSeek-V3.2 path (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51915">#51915</a>), AMD-Quark mixed-precision DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-MXFP4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57071">#57071</a>) and GLM-5.3-Flash Quark MXFP4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56176">#56176</a>) checkpoints, and a built-in <code>granite_thinking_parser</code> for Granite 4.2 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55957">#55957</a>).</li>
<li><strong>DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash</strong>: FlashMLA mega attention with the NVFP4 compressed KV cache as the SM100 default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56935">#56935</a>), DeepGEMM sparse MQA logits in the indexer (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56254">#56254</a>), Mega-Gate (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56266">#56266</a>), fused TP all-reduce + mHC input preparation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57643">#57643</a>) with the MoE finalize folded in (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58586">#58586</a>), fused small-batch WO-A (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58634">#58634</a>), MXFP8 <code>wo_b</code> GEMM + reduce-scatter (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" Engram <code>wkv</code> sharded across TP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58678">#58678</a>), serialized Engram lookups with huge-page host tables (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56926">#56926</a>), Engram tables shared across DP replicas by default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" NVFP4 cache gathers (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57604">#57604</a>), the fused query RMSNorm + MXFP8 path restored (<a class="issue-link href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57654">#57654</a>).</li>
<li><strong>DeepSeek V4</strong>: FlashInfer sparse MLA with fused inverse RoPE + FP8 quant (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58621">#58621</a>), stacked DSpark context WKV projections (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54674">#54674</a>), fused MoE expert distribution computed from the EP group (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57465">#57465</a>), FIM completion via <code>suffix</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/44229">#44229</a>), missing <code>string=</code> in tool calls parsed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56271">#56271</a>), request tools attached to an existing system message (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51856">#51856</a>), image block spacing preserved (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56882">#56882</a>), and DSpark width separated from MTP stage validation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54631">#54631</a>).</li>
<li><strong>GLM-5.3-Flash</strong>: opt-in FlashAttention and FlashMLA sparse backends on SM90 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55385">#55385</a>), NoPE sparse MLA on the FlashInfer SM120 backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55277">#55277</a>), cooperative top-k for small decode batches (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57327">#57327</a>), indexer decode workspace sized by pooled length (3 GiB saved, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57701">#57701</a>), metadata ops 1.6-4.8x faster (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58450">#58450</a>), fused kpool tail slot mapping (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57534">#57534</a>), kpool top-k through the shared dispatcher (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57546">#57546</a>), lower sparse MLA preparation overhead (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57458">#57458</a>), no D2H sync in the SM90 sparse MLA plan under async scheduling (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58684">#58684</a>), FlashKDA keeping the recurrent state in FP32 for long prefills (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58846">#58846</a>), and correctness fixes for kpool corruption with speculative decoding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58454">#58454</a>), SM90 <code>index_kpool</code> mismatch (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58704">#58704</a>), kpool tail strides (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57477">#57477</a>), 500k-token prompts (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57317">#57317</a>), dense MLP layers under sequence parallelism (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58061">#58061</a>), indexer top-k backend selection (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58594">#58594</a>) and varlen paged MQA launches (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55270">#55270</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Qwen3.8-Flash-Next (Qwen4Exp)</strong>: FP8 main KV cache on the QSA path (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55557">#55557</a>), FP8 TP with FlashInfer TRTLLM MoE (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55867">#55867</a>), SM90 QSA tuning (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57273">#57273</a>), fused HC down projection + SiLU (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58957">#58957</a>), lower PLE metadata overhead (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58114">#58114</a>), QSA indexer workspace fragmentation fixed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57105">#57105</a>), profiling KV cache released (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58961">#58961</a>), pinned PLE prefetch ids kept out of the CUDA graph pool (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58489">#58489</a>), and indexed expert-mapping lookups saving about 25 s of weight loading on DGX Spark (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58720">#58720</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Kimi K3 and MiniMax-M3</strong>: Kimi-K3 vision patch embedder as a GEMM (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58527">#58527</a>), fused KimiViT QK RoPE (up to 29x, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58651">#58651</a>), routed expert quantization (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57430">#57430</a>), reasoning parser (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57098">#57098</a>) and reasoning token counting (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58372">#58372</a>) fixes, DSpark context KV pointers refreshed after re-binding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58814">#58814</a>), stateless first chunks no longer classified as decodes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51483">#51483</a>), and Mamba block estimates that no longer stall admission on external prefix hits (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57050">#57050</a>); MiniMax-M3 encoder CUDA graphs (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58673">#58673</a>), <code>Conv3dLayer</code> patch embedding (about 62x, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58512">#58512</a>), <code>triton_mrope</code> in the vision tower (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58526">#58526</a>), fused MiniMax2 routing with non-unit scaling (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58880">#58880</a>), and processor fixes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58460">#58460</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59613">#59613</a>).</li>
<li><strong>DiffusionGemma</strong>: one-pass sampler statistics kernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58226">#58226</a>), <code>diffusion_constrained</code> reads over <code>logprob_token_ids</code> (about 25% faster, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58216">#58216</a>), fewer logit rows for prefill-only batches (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57416">#57416</a>), <code>logprob_token_ids</code> support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57417">#57417</a>), and fixes for concurrent logprobs (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57414">#57414</a>), eager fallback dtype (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57462">#57462</a>), multimodal inputs (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57589">#57589</a>), quantized LM heads (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/48521">#48521</a>) and CPU execution (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58964">#58964</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Multimodal</strong>: Triton <code>mm_input_norm</code> kernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56798">#56798</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56711">#56711</a>), raw pixels kept through the DP-sharded ViT path (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56872">#56872</a>), Qwen2.5-VL video fps honored for temporal M-RoPE (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57769">#57769</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56912">#56912</a>), Mistral3 placeholder grid (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53758">#53758</a>), Idefics3 unsplit patches (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" weights (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57487">#57487</a>), MiMo-V2.5 fused FP8 <code>qkv_proj</code> sharding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55911">#55911</a>) and buffer scalars (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54213">#54213</a>), Gemma4 FP8 KV with FA4 at head dim 512 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53175">#53175</a>), Laguna RoPE (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" <li><strong>LoRA</strong>: Nemotron VL language models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56231">#56231</a>), ModernBert (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57148">#57148</a>), VoyageQwen3 embeddings (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57708">#57708</a>), RoBERTa sequence classification (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58884">#58884</a>), and <code>modules_to_save</code> sequence-classification heads (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53555">#53555</a>) with per-adapter <code>num_labels</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57766">#57766</a>).</li>

<h2>Engine Core</h2>
<ul>
<li><strong>Model Runner V2</strong>: draft-model speculative decoding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/43091">#43091</a>), custom logits processors (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56497">#56497</a>) validated at admission (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57728">#57728</a>), randomized dummy inputs (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58411">#58411</a>), dummy tokens routed to MoE experts during profiling (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57270">#57270</a>), FULL decode graphs for one-token prompt tails (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58400">#58400</a>), token-to-request mappings shared (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57102">#57102</a>) and Mamba/GDN metadata reused across KV cache groups (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58762">#58762</a>), encoder-only ViT CUDA graphs (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56922">#56922</a>), weight offloader (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57834">#57834</a>) and pooling model (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57737">#57737</a>) released on shutdown, and fixes for multi-layer MTP KV in P/D (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55055">#55055</a>), fast-prefill with LoRA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56456">#56456</a>), stale block-table writes from dummy draft steps (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56734">#56734</a>), padded prompt tails in hybrid models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58434">#58434</a>), never-proposed draft slots (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58784">#58784</a>), auto-fit <code>max_model_len</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58149">#58149</a>) and <code>intermediate_tensors</code> during capture (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57745">#57745</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Speculative decoding</strong>: LiLiCorr drafter (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57934">#57934</a>), DFlash async scheduling (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58065">#58065</a>) and context K/V in the draft CUDA graph (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57632">#57632</a>), Gemma4 DSpark adaptive verification (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57263">#57263</a>), Kimi-K3 variable-length adaptive verification (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/52988">#52988</a>), CPU-GPU sync removed for heterogeneous vocabularies (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57396">#57396</a>), Triton recompiles avoided in the acceptance estimator (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57107">#57107</a>), and fixes for EAGLE/dense drafts with EP (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56930">#56930</a>), DFlash/DSpark profiling batches (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56448">#56448</a>), GLM MTP head memory (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55442">#55442</a>), prompt embeddings with drafts (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57356">#57356</a>), and TritonMLA causal multi-token decode (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51065">#51065</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Scheduler</strong>: <code>--max-num-active-seqs</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56758">#56758</a>), adaptive <code>--long-prefill-token-threshold</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57951">#57951</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58459">#58459</a>), <code>skipped_waiting</code> replaced by a KV-holding waiting queue (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58947">#58947</a>), atomic admission of <code>n &gt; 1</code> requests (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53936">#53936</a>), KV connector + MTP deadlock (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57104">#57104</a>), a throughput cliff when <code>max_num_seqs</code> is not a multiple of 8 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57355">#57355</a>), streaming continuations keeping logprobs and refreshing max tokens (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57447">#57447</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57676">#57676</a>), a resumable request + async scheduling race (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58259">#58259</a>), and a non-blocking structured-output grammar poll (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55931">#55931</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Prefix caching and hybrid models</strong>: extra keys tagged by source (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51899">#51899</a>) and LoRA paths hashed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59335">#59335</a>), <code>--enable-mamba-shared-prefix-checkpoint</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57382">#57382</a>), prompt-tail hits with MTP restored (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58368">#58368</a>), prompt-end checkpoints kept under sparse retention (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/59146">#59146</a>), align-mode checkpoint reservation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" <li><strong>Startup and memory</strong>: parallel Triton warmup compilation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58582">#58582</a>), JIT warmup disabled under <code>--enforce-eager</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58197">#58197</a>) unless fault tolerance is on (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58593">#58593</a>), DeepGEMM warmup reusing the MoE workspace (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57268">#57268</a>), allocator fragmentation no longer shrinking the KV cache during profiling (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58430">#58430</a>), sparse prefill buffers reserved before KV sizing (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57575">#57575</a>), stale FlashMLA workspace views released (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/56902">#56902</a>), DeepGEMM FP8 workspace halved (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53914">#53914</a>), shared Marlin/Humming workspaces (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57421">#57421</a>), FlashInfer BF16 MoE weights converted in place (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/54699">#54699</a>), UniProc startup threads bounded by the CPU quota (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/58946">#58946</a>), and runtime threads set before profiling (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/55891">#55891</a>).</li>
